My mother literally had to stand over me and check my backpack every day just to keep me on track.\n\nDoctor: Since these attention and forgetfulness problems started in childhood, have they stayed fairly similar throughout your life, or have they clearly gotten worse recently compared with your usual baseline? If they worsened, when did that change begin?\n\nPatient: Throughout my adult life, I managed to hide these struggles pretty well by relying on very strict routines and keeping constant lists. However, they became much worse about a year and a quarter ago when my husband's health declined and I suddenly had to manage all of his complex appointments and medications.\n\nDoctor: Before about 15 months ago, were your attention and forgetfulness problems fairly stable for many years, and did they worsen only after the stress of caring for your husband? Or have they been progressively getting worse on their own regardless of stress or any medical illness?\n\nPatient: They were actually very stable for decades because my strict routines and lists kept me afloat; they didn't just get worse on their own. It was only when my husband's health declined about 15 months ago that the extra stress and paperwork completely overwhelmed my coping systems. Before that, I was managing to mask it all pretty well.\n\nDoctor: Before your husband’s illness, did these attention problems cause clear difficulties in more than one part of your life, such as work, managing the household, relationships, or finances? Can you give one or two examples?"
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Before my husband's illness, I avoided major difficulties as an adult by heavily masking my distractibility with intense written lists and a very meticulous approach to cooking, gardening, and church events. However, earlier in life during grade school, it did cause clear problems with my schoolwork. My teachers frequently noted that I was daydreaming, losing track of instructions, and forgetting homework unless my mother constantly supervised me.

past year I keep forgetting everyday things I’ve promised to do—calling the cardiologist back, picking up a prescription on the way home, even simple chores like putting the laundry in the dryer—so I end up apologizing a lot and double‑checking with people because I don’t trust my memory anymore.\n- S010 (Fidgeting): I’ve noticed that when I’m sitting through a long church meeting or waiting with my husband at the clinic, I’m always tapping my foot, rubbing my hands together, or shifting around in the chair without really meaning to, like I just can’t get fully comfortable staying still.\n- S011 (Leaving_Seat): During longer events, like Bible study or my husband’s appointments, I find myself getting up to straighten the coffee table, use the restroom, or “check on something,” because my backpack.\n- functional_impairment: Because I keep losing track of appointments and mixing up times or follow-up calls, my husband has almost missed important tests, and I’ve pulled back from volunteering at the senior center and church because I’m afraid I’ll let people down or make a serious mistake.\n- duration: These problems have really stood out and interfered with my day-to-day life Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, I’m glad you came in today. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Lately I keep forgetting everyday things—calls I promised to make, appointments I just wrote down, even simple errands—and it’s starting to cause problems with my husband’s care. My primary care doctor actually referred me here to help figure out if this is early dementia or perhaps a longstanding attention problem that is just now catching up with me.\n\nDoctor: Did you have similar problems with attention, forgetfulness, or being easily distracted when you were a child or teenager, such as at school or home? Or is this mainly a new change that started later in adulthood?\n\nPatient: You know, looking back, I realize these issues actually go all the way back to my childhood. Even in grade school, my teachers would write on my report cards that I was a bright girl but always daydreaming, losing track of instructions, and forgetting to turn in homework. My mother literally had to stand over me and check my backpack every day just to keep me on track.\n\nDoctor: Since these attention and forgetfulness problems started in childhood, have they stayed fairly similar throughout your life, or have they clearly gotten worse recently compared with your usual baseline? If they worsened, when did that change begin?\n\nPatient: Throughout my adult life, I managed to hide these struggles pretty well by relying on very strict routines and keeping constant lists. However, they became much worse about a year and a quarter ago when my husband's health declined and I suddenly had to manage all of his complex appointments and medications.\n\nDoctor: Before about 15 months ago, were your attention and forgetfulness problems fairly stable for many years, and did they worsen only after the stress of caring for your husband? Or have they been progressively getting worse on their own regardless of stress or any medical illness?\n\nPatient: They were actually very stable for decades because my strict routines and lists kept me afloat; they didn't just get worse on their own. It was only when my husband's health declined about 15 months ago that the extra stress and paperwork completely overwhelmed my coping systems. Before that, I was managing to mask it all pretty well.\n\nDoctor: Before your husband’s illness, did these attention problems cause clear difficulties in more than one part of your life, such as work, managing the household, relationships, or finances? Can you give one or two examples?\n\nPatient: Before my husband's illness, I avoided major difficulties as an adult by heavily masking my distractibility with intense written lists and a very meticulous approach to cooking, gardening, and church events. However, earlier in life during grade school, it did cause clear problems with my schoolwork. My teachers frequently noted that I was daydreaming, losing track of instructions, and forgetting homework unless my mother constantly supervised me.\n\nDoctor: Aside from school as a child, did these attention problems also clearly affect another setting before age 12, such as at home, with friends, or in activities? What kinds of problems did they cause there?\n\nPatient: Yes, those issues definitely affected my home life as well. My mother had to constantly stand over me, supervise me, and check my backpack to make sure I actually finished my homework and chores. Otherwise, I would completely lose track of instructions or forget what I was supposed to be doing.\n\nDoctor: As a child and later as an adult, were you mainly forgetful and easily distracted, or did you also often feel physically restless, fidgety, or act impulsively? Which pattern has been more consistent for you over your life?\n\nPatient: The forgetful, daydreaming, and easily distracted pattern has definitely been the most consistent throughout my life, going back to grade school where I was always losing track of instructions. While I do experience some physical restlessness now—like tapping my foot or needing to get up during long church meetings—the inattentive side is what I've had to mask with lists and routines my entire life.\n\nDoctor: As an adult before your husband became ill, did your lifelong attention problems clearly cause impairment in at least two settings, such as work, home, finances, or relationships? What is one specific example from another setting besides home?"

Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, I’m glad you came in today. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Lately I keep forgetting everyday things—calls I promised to make, appointments I just wrote down, even simple errands—and it’s starting to cause problems with my husband’s care. My primary care doctor actually referred me here to help figure out if this is early dementia or perhaps a longstanding attention problem that is just now catching up with me.\n\nDoctor: Did you have similar problems with attention, forgetfulness, or being easily distracted when you were a child or teenager, such as at school or home? Or is this mainly a new change that started later in adulthood?\n\nPatient: You know, looking back, I realize these issues actually go all the way back to my childhood. Even in grade school, my teachers would write on my report cards that I was a bright girl but always daydreaming, losing track of instructions, and forgetting to turn in homework. My mother literally had to stand over me and check my backpack every day just to keep me on track.\n\nDoctor: Since these attention and forgetfulness problems started in childhood, have they stayed fairly similar throughout your life, or have they clearly gotten worse recently compared with your usual baseline? If they worsened, when did that change begin?\n\nPatient: Throughout my adult life, I managed to hide these struggles pretty well by relying on very strict routines and keeping constant lists. However, they became much worse about a year and a quarter ago when my husband's health declined and I suddenly had to manage all of his complex appointments and medications.\n\nDoctor: Before about 15 months ago, were your attention and forgetfulness problems fairly stable for many years, and did they worsen only after the stress of caring for your husband? Or have they been progressively getting worse on their own regardless of stress or any medical illness?\n\nPatient: They were actually very stable for decades because my strict routines and lists kept me afloat; they didn't just get worse on their own. It was only when my husband's health declined about 15 months ago that the extra stress and paperwork completely overwhelmed my coping systems. Before that, I was managing to mask it all pretty well.\n\nDoctor: Before your husband’s illness, did these attention problems cause clear difficulties in more than one part of your life, such as work, managing the household, relationships, or finances? Can you give one or two examples?\n\nPatient: Before my husband's illness, I avoided major difficulties as an adult by heavily masking my distractibility with intense written lists and a very meticulous approach to cooking, gardening, and church events. However, earlier in life during grade school, it did cause clear problems with my schoolwork. My teachers frequently noted that I was daydreaming, losing track of instructions, and forgetting homework unless my mother constantly supervised me.\n\nDoctor: Aside from school as a child, did these attention problems also clearly affect another setting before age 12, such as at home, with friends, or in activities? What kinds of problems did they cause there?\n\nPatient: Yes, those issues definitely affected my home life as well. My mother had to constantly stand over me, supervise me, and check my backpack to make sure I actually finished my homework and chores. Otherwise, I would completely lose track of instructions or forget what I was supposed to be doing.\n\nDoctor: As a child and later as an adult, were you mainly forgetful and easily distracted, or did you also often feel physically restless, fidgety, or act impulsively? Which pattern has been more consistent for you over your life?\n\nPatient: The forgetful, daydreaming, and easily distracted pattern has definitely been the most consistent throughout my life, going back to grade school where I was always losing track of instructions. While I do experience some physical restlessness now—like tapping my foot or needing to get up during long church meetings—the inattentive side is what I've had to mask with lists and routines my entire life.\n\nDoctor: As an adult before your husband became ill, did your lifelong attention problems clearly cause impairment in at least two settings, such as work, home, finances, or relationships? What is one specific example from another setting besides home?\n\nPatient: Before my husband got sick, I didn't really feel like my attention problems caused clear impairment in other settings because I worked so hard to mask them. I relied heavily on written lists and a very meticulous approach to manage my cooking, gardening, and church volunteering. Because of those systems, it didn't really cause obvious problems in other areas of my life back then.\n\nDoctor: Even if you masked it well, before your husband became ill did these attention problems still lead to any clear mistakes or missed responsibilities in another setting besides home, such as work, church volunteering, finances, or relationships? Please give one specific example if so."

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Lately I keep forgetting everyday things—calls I promised to make, appointments I just wrote down, even simple errands—and it’s starting to cause problems with my husband’s care. My primary care doctor actually referred me here to help figure out if this is early dementia or perhaps a longstanding attention problem that is just now catching up with me.\n\nDoctor: Did you have similar problems with attention, forgetfulness, or being easily distracted when you were a child or teenager, such as at school or home? Or is this mainly a new change that started later in adulthood?\n\nPatient: You know, looking back, I realize these issues actually go all the way back to my childhood. Even in grade school, my teachers would write on my report cards that I was a bright girl but always daydreaming, losing track of instructions, and forgetting to turn in homework. My mother literally had to stand over me and check my backpack every day just to keep me on track.\n\nDoctor: Since these attention and forgetfulness problems started in childhood, have they stayed fairly similar throughout your life, or have they clearly gotten worse recently compared with your usual baseline? If they worsened, when did that change begin?\n\nPatient: Throughout my adult life, I managed to hide these struggles pretty well by relying on very strict routines and keeping constant lists. However, they became much worse about a year and a quarter ago when my husband's health declined and I suddenly had to manage all of his complex appointments and medications.\n\nDoctor: Before about 15 months ago, were your attention and forgetfulness problems fairly stable for many years, and did they worsen only after the stress of caring for your husband? Or have they been progressively getting worse on their own regardless of stress or any medical illness?\n\nPatient: They were actually very stable for decades because my strict routines and lists kept me afloat; they didn't just get worse on their own. It was only when my husband's health declined about 15 months ago that the extra stress and paperwork completely overwhelmed my coping systems. However, earlier in life during grade school, it did cause clear problems with my schoolwork. My teachers frequently noted that I was daydreaming, losing track of instructions, and forgetting homework unless my mother constantly supervised me.\n\nDoctor: Aside from school as a child, did these attention problems also clearly affect another setting before age 12, such as at home, with friends, or in activities? What kinds of problems did they cause there?\n\nPatient: Yes, those issues definitely affected my home life as well. My mother had to constantly stand over me, supervise me, and check my backpack to make sure I actually finished my homework and chores. Otherwise, I would completely lose track of instructions or forget what I was supposed to be doing.\n\nDoctor: As a child and later as an adult, were you mainly forgetful and easily distracted, or did you also often feel physically restless, fidgety, or act impulsively? Which pattern has been more consistent for you over your life?\n\nPatient: The forgetful, daydreaming, and easily distracted pattern has definitely been the most consistent throughout my life, going back to grade school where I was always losing track of instructions. While I do experience some physical restlessness now—like tapping my foot or needing to get up during long church meetings—the inattentive side is what I've had to mask with lists and routines my entire life.\n\nDoctor: As an adult before your husband became ill, did your lifelong attention problems clearly cause impairment in at least two settings, such as work, home, finances, or relationships? What is one specific example from another setting besides home?\n\nPatient: Before my husband got sick, I didn't really feel like my attention problems caused clear impairment in other settings because I worked so hard to mask them. I relied heavily on written lists and a very meticulous approach to manage my cooking, gardening, and church volunteering. Because of those systems, it didn't really cause obvious problems in other areas of my life back then.\n\nDoctor: Even if you masked it well, before your husband became ill did these attention problems still lead to any clear mistakes or missed responsibilities in another setting besides home, such as work, church volunteering, finances, or relationships? Please give one specific example if so.\n\nPatient: Before my husband got sick, I managed to avoid making major mistakes in my church volunteering or other activities by being extremely meticulous and relying heavily on written lists to mask my distractibility. However, that underlying struggle has always been there under the surface, much like during my school years when my teachers constantly noted that I was daydreaming, losing track of instructions, and forgetting my homework.\n\nDoctor: When you were a child or teenager, besides school and home, did your attention problems also cause clear trouble in another setting like friendships, hobbies, or part-time jobs? Please give one specific example."

Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight. Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight. Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world. Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.
